\section{Local fiber spectra and the critical Dirichlet law}
\label{sec:local-spectrum}

The pressure records only exponential growth.  On the proved frozen
half-line, a finer limit survives without exponential normalization.  It is
controlled by the fixed-level stabilization in the preceding section.

For \(m\ge1\) and \(k\ge1\), put
\[
 C_m(k):=\card{\{x\in X_m:d_m(x)=k\}},
 \qquad
 \nu_m:=\sum_{k\ge1}C_m(k)\delta_k.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Local microcanonical spectrum]
\label{thm:local-fiber-spectrum}
For every \(m\ge1\),
\[
 C_m(1)=2.
\]
The two singleton fibers have residues
\(F_{m+1}-1\) and \(F_{m+2}-1\).  For every \(k\ge2\),
\[
 C_m(k)\le4\Psi(k),
 \qquad
 C_m(k)=4\Psi(k)\quad(m\ge2k).
\]
In particular, \(C_m(2)=4\) for \(m\ge4\).  Moreover, the locally finite
measures \(\nu_m\) converge vaguely on the discrete space \(\mathbb N\) to
\[
 \nu_\infty=2\delta_1+4\sum_{k\ge2}\Psi(k)\delta_k.
\]
Equivalently, the masses of every finite subset of \(\mathbb N\) eventually
stabilize.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The fiber--partition interval identity gives, as multisets, the values of
\(R\) on the disjoint consecutive layers \(\mathcal I_m\) and
\(\mathcal I_{m+1}\).  Hence
\begin{equation}
 C_m(k)=N_m(k)+N_{m+1}(k).
 \label{eq:fiber-level-two-layer-count}
\end{equation}
The uniform layer-count lemma gives one value equal to \(1\) in each layer,
the bound \(N_j(k)\le2\Psi(k)\) for \(k\ge2\), and equality for
\(j\ge2k\).  Summing the two layers proves all the asserted counts.  Under
the interval identity, the two level-one points are the left endpoints
\(F_{m+1}-1\) and \(F_{m+2}-1\); inspection of the two cases in
\Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval} shows that these are also their fiber
residues.  Finally, every compact subset of the discrete space
\(\mathbb N\) is finite, and each of its point masses eventually has the
displayed value.  This is precisely vague convergence.
\end{proof}

Define the unnormalized limiting spectral partition function by
\[
 \mathcal Z(s):=\frac{4}{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}-2
 \qquad(s>\sigma_0).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Exact negative-tilt spectral limit]
\label{thm:spectral-partition-function}
For every \(s>\sigma_0\),
\[
 \lim_{m\to\infty}S_{-s}(m)=\mathcal Z(s)<\infty.
\]
For an arbitrary real tilt \(t\), the exact dichotomy is
\[
 \begin{cases}
 S_t(m)\longrightarrow
 \displaystyle\frac{4}{2-\zeta(-t-1)/\zeta(-t)}-2,&t< -\sigma_0,\\[8pt]
 S_t(m)\longrightarrow+\infty,&t\ge-\sigma_0.
 \end{cases}
\]
Thus \(-\sigma_0\) is the convergence boundary for the unnormalized
partition functions, independently of whether it is the boundary of the
zero-pressure set.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(s>\sigma_0\), \Cref{thm:local-fiber-spectrum} and dominated convergence
give
\[
 S_{-s}(m)
 =\sum_{k\ge1}C_m(k)k^{-s}
 \longrightarrow
 2+4\sum_{k\ge2}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^s}.
\]
The domination follows from \(C_m(k)\le4\Psi(k)\) for \(k\ge2\), and the
majorant is summable by
\eqref{eq:weinstein-level-dirichlet-series}.  Weinstein's Dirichlet identity
then reduces the last expression to \(\mathcal Z(s)\).

Suppose next that \(0<s\le\sigma_0\).  If \(2<s\le\sigma_0\), the geometric
series in \eqref{eq:weinstein-level-dirichlet-series} diverges.  If
\(0<s\le2\), the one-factor generating words give
\(\Psi(k)\ge\phi_{\rm E}(k)\), and the totient Dirichlet series diverges.
Thus \(\sum_{k\ge2}\Psi(k)k^{-s}=+\infty\) throughout this range.  For each
fixed \(K\), \Cref{thm:local-fiber-spectrum} gives, once \(m\ge2K\),
\[
 S_{-s}(m)\ge2+4\sum_{2\le k\le K}\Psi(k)k^{-s}.
\]
Letting \(K\to\infty\) proves divergence for
\(-\sigma_0\le t<0\).  For \(t\ge0\), nonempty fibers satisfy
\(d_m(x)^t\ge1\), and hence
\(S_t(m)\ge\card{X_m}=F_{m+2}\to\infty\).
\end{proof}

The singularity of \(\mathcal Z\) also determines a limiting Gibbs law.  Put
\[
 \kappa:=-\left.\frac{d}{ds}\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}
          \right|_{s=\sigma_0}
 =\sum_{n\ge2}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(n)\log n}{n^{\sigma_0}}
 =2.589184379946924\ldots.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Critical Gibbs spectrum]
\label{thm:critical-gibbs-spectrum}
As \(s\downarrow\sigma_0\),
\[
 \mathcal Z(s)=\frac{4}{\kappa(s-\sigma_0)}+O(1).
\]
For \(s>\sigma_0\), define
\[
 Q_{m,s}(k):=\frac{C_m(k)k^{-s}}{S_{-s}(m)}.
\]
Then \(Q_{m,s}\) converges in total variation to the probability measure
\[
 Q_s(1)=\frac2{\mathcal Z(s)},
 \qquad
 Q_s(k)=\frac{4\Psi(k)k^{-s}}{\mathcal Z(s)}\quad(k\ge2).
\]
In particular,
\[
 Q_s(1)\sim\frac\kappa2(s-\sigma_0).
\]
If \(K\) has law \(Q_s\), then
\[
 \mathbb E_s[\log K]
 =\frac1{s-\sigma_0}+O(1),
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Var}_s(\log K)
 =\frac1{(s-\sigma_0)^2}+O(1).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The totient series is absolutely differentiable in a neighborhood of
\(\sigma_0>2\), and therefore
\[
 2-\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}
 =\kappa(s-\sigma_0)+O((s-\sigma_0)^2).
\]
The pole expansion and the asymptotic for \(Q_s(1)\) follow.  For fixed
\(s>\sigma_0\), the pointwise convergence and summable domination used in
\Cref{thm:spectral-partition-function} give convergence of the unnormalized
weights in \(\ell^1(\mathbb N)\).  Normalizing therefore gives total
variation convergence.

Absolute differentiation of the limiting Dirichlet series yields
\[
 -\frac{d}{ds}\log\mathcal Z(s)=\mathbb E_s[\log K],
 \qquad
 \frac{d^2}{ds^2}\log\mathcal Z(s)
 =\operatorname{Var}_s(\log K).
\]
Since the denominator defining \(\mathcal Z\) is analytic at \(\sigma_0\)
and has a simple zero there, its Taylor expansion gives
\[
 \log\mathcal Z(s)
 =-\log(s-\sigma_0)+\log(4/\kappa)+O(s-\sigma_0),
\]
where the remainder is analytic.  Differentiating this local analytic
expansion proves the two moment estimates.
\end{proof}

The same pole has a Tauberian consequence for the generating-orbit counts.

\begin{theorem}[Generating-orbit Tauberian law]
\label{thm:psi-tauberian}
As \(x\to\infty\),
\[
 \sum_{k\le x}\Psi(k)
 \sim\frac{x^{\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0\kappa},
 \qquad
 \sum_{k\le x}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^{\sigma_0}}
 \sim\frac1\kappa\log x.
\]
Consequently,
\[
 \left(\frac4\kappa+o(1)\right)\log m
 \le S_{-\sigma_0}(m)
 \le\left(\frac{2\log\varphi}{\kappa}+o(1)\right)m.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Set
\[
 A(s)=\sum_{n\ge2}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(n)}{n^s}
 =\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}-1.
\]
For \(\Re s>\sigma_0\), Weinstein's identity reads
\[
 1+\sum_{k\ge2}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^s}=\frac1{1-A(s)}.
\]
On the line \(\Re s=\sigma_0\), if \(\tau\ne0\), then
\[
 |A(\sigma_0+i\tau)|<A(\sigma_0)=1.
\]
Indeed, equality in the triangle inequality would force all
\(n^{-i\tau}\) with \(n\ge2\) to have the same phase; since the sum would
have to equal the positive number \(1\), it would in particular force
\(2^{-i\tau}=3^{-i\tau}=1\).  This contradicts
\(\log2/\log3\notin\mathbb Q\).  Thus the Dirichlet series has no other
boundary singularity and has at \(s=\sigma_0\) a simple pole of residue
\(1/\kappa\).  The Wiener--Ikehara theorem
\cite{Korevaar2004} now gives
\[
 \sum_{k\le x}\Psi(k)
 \sim\frac{x^{\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0\kappa}.
\]
Partial summation gives the second asymptotic.

For the critical finite-window sum, stabilization gives
\[
 S_{-\sigma_0}(m)
 \ge2+4\sum_{2\le k\le m/2}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^{\sigma_0}},
\]
which proves the lower bound.  The uniform layer estimate and the absence of
levels above \(D_m\) give
\[
 S_{-\sigma_0}(m)
 \le2+4\sum_{2\le k\le D_m}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^{\sigma_0}}.
\]
Now use the second Tauberian asymptotic and
\(\log D_m\sim\tfrac12m\log\varphi\).
\end{proof}

Finally, the same estimates narrow the possible location of the zero-pressure
boundary.  Write \(p_m(t)=m^{-1}\log S_t(m)\), and set
\[
 \beta_*:=\frac{\log\varphi}{\log(2/\varphi)}
 =2.270559453959664\ldots.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Pressure bounds in the transition strip]
\label{thm:pressure-transition-strip}
For \(0<s<\sigma_0\),
\[
 \max\left\{0,\log\varphi-s\log\frac2\varphi\right\}
 \le\liminf_{m\to\infty}p_m(-s)
\]
and
\[
 \limsup_{m\to\infty}p_m(-s)
 \le\min\left\{\log\varphi,
       \frac{\sigma_0-s}{2}\log\varphi\right\}.
\]
In particular,
\[
 \liminf_{m\to\infty}p_m(t)>0\qquad(t>-\beta_*).
\]
Together with \Cref{thm:negative-temperature-freezing}, this confines any
boundary of the zero-pressure set to
\[
 [-\sigma_0,-\beta_*]
 =[-2.478750785733960\ldots,-2.270559453959664\ldots].
\]
The width of this candidate strip is
\[
 \sigma_0-\beta_*=0.208191331774296\ldots.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under the uniform distribution on \(X_m\), the mean fiber multiplicity is
\(2^m/F_{m+2}\).  Since \(u\mapsto u^{-s}\) is convex, Jensen's inequality
gives
\[
 \frac{S_{-s}(m)}{F_{m+2}}
 \ge\left(\frac{2^m}{F_{m+2}}\right)^{-s}.
\]
After taking logarithms and dividing by \(m\), this gives the nonzero term in
the lower bound; the term zero follows from the two singleton fibers.

For the upper bound, fix \(a>\sigma_0\).  Since all occurring levels satisfy
\(k\le D_m\), \Cref{thm:local-fiber-spectrum} gives
\[
 S_{-s}(m)
 \le2+4D_m^{a-s}\sum_{k\ge2}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^a}.
\]
Using \(m^{-1}\log D_m\to\tfrac12\log\varphi\) and then letting
\(a\downarrow\sigma_0\) proves the second term in the upper bound.  The
first follows directly from \(d_m(x)^{-s}\le1\).  If
\(-\beta_*<t<0\), the lower bound is strictly positive by the definition of
\(\beta_*\); if \(t\ge0\), then \(S_t(m)\ge F_{m+2}\).  This proves the
last assertion without assuming that the pressure limit exists.
\end{proof}

The remaining transition strip admits an exact renewal description.  For a
reduced fraction \(p/q\in(0,1)\), write its unique negative continued fraction
as
\[
 \frac pq=
 \cfrac1{a_1-\cfrac1{a_2-\ddots-\cfrac1{a_r}}},
 \qquad a_i\ge2,
\]
and set
\[
 d(p/q):=\sum_{i=1}^r(a_i-1),
 \qquad c(p/q):=2d(p/q)+1.
\]
For a finite vector \(\mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_r)\), define its negative
continuant by
\[
 \begin{gathered}
  \mathcal D(\varnothing)=1,\qquad \mathcal D(a_1)=a_1,\\
  \mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_r)
  =a_r\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_{r-1})
   -\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_{r-2}).
 \end{gathered}
\]
Thus the denominator of the displayed negative continued fraction is
\(\mathcal D(a_1,\ldots,a_r)\).

For real \(s\), put
\begin{equation}
 b_\ell(s):=
 \sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\c(p/q)=\ell}}q^{-s},
 \qquad
 u_0(s):=1,\qquad
 u_j(s):=\sum_{\ell=1}^j b_\ell(s)u_{j-\ell}(s).
 \label{eq:weighted-renewal-coefficients}
\end{equation}
Every sum defining \(b_\ell(s)\) is finite.  More explicitly,
\begin{equation}
 b_{2d+1}(s)
 =\sum_{r=1}^d
   \sum_{\substack{e_1+\cdots+e_r=d\\e_i\ge1}}
   \mathcal D(e_1+1,\ldots,e_r+1)^{-s},
 \qquad b_{2d}(s)=0.
 \label{eq:weighted-letter-coefficients}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Weighted generator renewal]
\label{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}
For \(j\ge1\), let
\[
 \Lambda_j(s):=\sum_{n\in\mathcal I_j}R(n)^{-s}.
\]
Then, for every real \(s\),
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda_j(s)
 =1+u_j(s)+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{j-1}u_\ell(s)
 =2\sum_{\ell=0}^{j-1}u_\ell(s)+u_j(s)-1.
 \label{eq:weighted-layer-renewal}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every \(m\ge1\),
\begin{equation}
 S_{-s}(m)
 =4\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(s)+3u_m(s)+u_{m+1}(s)-2.
 \label{eq:weighted-window-renewal}
\end{equation}
Moreover, with formal power series
\[
 B_s(z):=\sum_{\ell\ge1}b_\ell(s)z^\ell,
 \qquad U_s(z):=\sum_{j\ge0}u_j(s)z^j,
\]
one has
\begin{equation}
 U_s(z)=\frac1{1-B_s(z)}.
 \label{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We use the generating-number parameterization in
\cite[Secs.~3--4]{Weinstein2016}, but not its layer-stabilization theorem.
Under Weinstein's free-monoid bijection, a generating number \(g\) corresponds
uniquely to a nonempty word
\[
 w=\frac{p_1}{q_1}\times\cdots\times\frac{p_r}{q_r}
\]
in reduced fractions from \((0,1)\).  Proposition~3.3 and formula~(16) of
that paper give, in the present notation,
\begin{equation}
 R(g)=\prod_{i=1}^r q_i,
 \qquad L(g)=\sum_{i=1}^r c(p_i/q_i).
 \label{eq:generator-weight-length}
\end{equation}
The continued-fraction bijection also gives
\eqref{eq:weighted-letter-coefficients}.  Unique decomposition of a free word
therefore shows that \(u_j(s)\) is exactly the sum of \(R(g)^{-s}\) over all
generating numbers with \(L(g)=j\), and proves
\eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function} as a formal identity.

It remains to count each generating orbit in a fixed layer.  A generating
number belongs to Weinstein's odd first-index sheet, so its initial-block
parameter is \(1\).  The formulas already established in
\eqref{eq:L-a-shift}--\eqref{eq:L-sigma-shift} consequently specialize to
\[
 L([a]g)=L(g)+2a,\qquad
 L([a]\tau g)=L(g)+1+2a,\qquad
 L(\sigma n)=L(n)+1
\]
for \(a\ge0\).  Hence the two branches in Weinstein's first sheet contribute
one point to every layer \(j\ge L(g)\); their sigma images contribute one
further point precisely when \(j>L(g)\).  The action is free and distinct
generating orbits are disjoint.  Since \(\mathcal I_j\) contains exactly one
point of partition multiplicity \(1\) for \(j\ge1\), summing the orbit weights
gives \eqref{eq:weighted-layer-renewal}.  The two-layer identity
\eqref{eq:real-collision-two-layers} then gives
\eqref{eq:weighted-window-renewal}.  Notice that the restriction \(j\ge1\) is
essential in \eqref{eq:weighted-layer-renewal}, because \(\mathcal I_0\) is
empty.
\end{proof}

Let \(\mathfrak P\) denote the Stern--Brocot pressure
\[
 \mathfrak P(\theta)
 :=\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac1d
   \log\sum_{T\in\mathcal T_d}|T|^\theta .
\]

\begin{lemma}[New-denominator layer estimate]
\label{lem:knauf-layer-estimate}
For every fixed real \(s<2\),
\begin{equation}
 a_d(s):=
 \sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\d(p/q)=d}}q^{-s}
 \asymp_s \exp\{d\mathfrak P(s/2)\}.
 \label{eq:knauf-layer-pressure}
\end{equation}
For every fixed \(s\ge2\), one has instead
\[
 \lim_{d\to\infty}\frac1d\log a_d(s)=0.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First suppose \(0<s<2\), and put \(\beta=s/2\).  The even Knauf
partition function \(Z^{K,e}_d(2\beta)\) is exactly \(a_d(s)\): its
states are the new Stern--Brocot denominators at depth \(d\).  In the
notation of Fiala, Kleban, and \"Ozl\"uk, the first-coordinate identity
for their Knauf vector, its Perron--Frobenius iteration, and its spectral
asymptotic are given in
\cite[Sec.~IV, especially Eqs.~(23), (26), and (28)]{FialaKlebanOzluk2003}.
They give
\[
 a_d(s)=c_s\lambda(s/2)^d(1+o(1)),\qquad c_s>0.
\]
Their free-energy identification, together with the denominator formula
for Stern--Brocot pressure
\cite[Thm.~1.1 and the denominator formula in Sec.~2]{KessebohmerStratmann2007},
gives \(\log\lambda(s/2)=\mathfrak P(s/2)\).  This proves
\eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} in the range \(0<s<2\).  At \(s=0\),
there are exactly \(2^{d-1}\) new denominators and
\(\mathfrak P(0)=\log2\), so the assertion is immediate.

It remains to supply the estimate for \(s<0\), a range not covered by
the positive-temperature Perron--Frobenius statement just cited.  Put
\(t=-s>0\), and let
\[
 L=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 R=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \lVert M\rVert_\Sigma=\sum_{i,j=1}^2M_{ij}.
\]
The Stern--Brocot recursion gives, by induction, a bijection between the
new denominators at depth \(d\) and words \(w\in\{L,R\}^{d-1}\), under
which
\begin{equation}
 q(w)=\lVert M_w\rVert_\Sigma.
 \label{eq:stern-brocot-matrix-denominator}
\end{equation}
Define
\[
 Z_n(t):=\sum_{w\in\{L,R\}^n}\lVert M_w\rVert_\Sigma^t,
 \qquad n\ge0.
\]
For nonnegative matrices \(A,B\),
\(\lVert AB\rVert_\Sigma\le
\lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma\), and hence
\begin{equation}
 Z_{m+n}(t)\le Z_m(t)Z_n(t).
 \label{eq:matrix-partition-submultiplicative}
\end{equation}
For the reverse inequality with one bridging letter, write
\(\alpha=A^T(1,1)^T\) and \(\beta=B(1,1)^T\).  Direct multiplication gives
\[
 \lVert ALB\rVert_\Sigma+\lVert ARB\rVert_\Sigma
 =\lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma
   +\alpha_1\beta_1+\alpha_2\beta_2.
\]
Thus at least one \(C\in\{L,R\}\) satisfies
\(\lVert ACB\rVert_\Sigma\ge
\frac12\lVert A\rVert_\Sigma\lVert B\rVert_\Sigma\).  Summing over
all pairs of words yields
\begin{equation}
 Z_{m+n+1}(t)\ge2^{-t}Z_m(t)Z_n(t).
 \label{eq:matrix-partition-bridged}
\end{equation}

Let \(\lambda_t=\lim_n Z_n(t)^{1/n}\), whose existence follows from
\eqref{eq:matrix-partition-submultiplicative}.  That inequality gives
\(Z_n(t)\ge\lambda_t^n\).  Iterating
\eqref{eq:matrix-partition-bridged} \(k-1\) times and then letting
\(k\to\infty\) gives
\[
 Z_{k(n+1)-1}(t)\ge2^{-t(k-1)}Z_n(t)^k,
 \qquad
 Z_n(t)\le2^t\lambda_t^{n+1}.
\]
Consequently \(Z_n(t)\asymp_t\lambda_t^n\).  By
\eqref{eq:stern-brocot-matrix-denominator} and the denominator form of
Stern--Brocot pressure cited above,
\(\log\lambda_t=\mathfrak P(-t/2)\).  Since
\(a_d(-t)=Z_{d-1}(t)\), this proves
\eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} for \(s<0\).

Finally, the logarithmic assertion for \(s\ge2\) is precisely the
denominator formulation of the zero-pressure part of
\cite[Thm.~1.1]{KessebohmerStratmann2007}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[All-real pressure and the freezing corner]
\label{thm:exact-negative-pressure}
\label{thm:all-real-pressure}
For every \(s\in\mathbb R\), the radius of convergence of \(B_s\) is
\begin{equation}
 R_B(s)=\exp\{-\mathfrak P(s/2)/2\}.
 \label{eq:Bs-radius}
\end{equation}
Thus \(R_B(s)<1\) for \(s<2\), and \(R_B(s)=1\) for \(s\ge2\).
The nonnegative real roots of \(B_s(r)=1\) are classified as follows:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
s<\sigma_0 & \text{a unique root }r_s\in(0,R_B(s)),\\
s=\sigma_0 & \text{the unique root }r_s=1=R_B(s),\\
s>\sigma_0 & \text{no root in }[0,R_B(s)].
\end{array}
\]
The pressure exists for every real tilt and satisfies
\begin{equation}
 P(-s):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log S_{-s}(m)
 =\begin{cases}
   -\log r_s,&s<\sigma_0,\\
   0,&s\ge\sigma_0.
  \end{cases}
 \label{eq:all-real-pressure}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for \(t>-\sigma_0\), \(P(t)>0\) is the unique solution of
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{q=2}^{\infty}
 \sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
 q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}=1,
 \label{eq:exact-negative-pressure}
\end{equation}
whereas \(P(t)=0\) for \(t\le-\sigma_0\).  In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \{t\in\mathbb R:P(t)>0\}=(-\sigma_0,\infty).
 \label{eq:exact-zero-pressure-set}
\end{equation}

The restriction of \(P\) to \((-\sigma_0,\infty)\) is real analytic and
strictly convex.  At \(t_c=-\sigma_0\), it has the finite one-sided
derivatives
\begin{equation}
 P'_-(t_c)=0,
 \qquad
 P'_+(t_c)=
 \frac{\displaystyle
  \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
       q^{-\sigma_0}\log q}
 {\displaystyle
  \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
       c(p/q)q^{-\sigma_0}}>0.
 \label{eq:critical-pressure-slopes}
\end{equation}
Consequently, \(P\) is not differentiable at the freezing point.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write \(a_d(s):=b_{2d+1}(s)\).  The negative-continued-fraction
parameterization gives
\[
 a_d(s)=\sum_{\substack{0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\\d(p/q)=d}}q^{-s}.
\]
There are \(2^{d-1}\) fractions in this layer, and their denominators satisfy
\(d+1\le q\le F_{d+2}\).  In particular, every \(B_s\) has positive finite
radius.  The layer is precisely the new-denominator layer of the Knauf model.
The precise estimate in \Cref{lem:knauf-layer-estimate} gives, for fixed
\(s<2\), \eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure}, and gives
\(d^{-1}\log a_d(s)\to0\) for \(s\ge2\).  Cauchy--Hadamard now proves
\eqref{eq:Bs-radius}.  Moreover, \eqref{eq:knauf-layer-pressure} shows that
the terms of \(B_s\) do not tend to zero on \(|z|=R_B(s)\) when \(s<2\).
At \(s=2\), the positive real boundary also diverges.  For \(s>2\), Tonelli's
theorem gives
\begin{equation}
 B_s(1)=\sum_{q\ge2}\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(q)}{q^s}
 =\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}-1<\infty.
 \label{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one}
\end{equation}

The coefficients are nonnegative and the support contains both \(3\) and
\(5\), so \(B_s\) is strictly increasing on its positive interval of
convergence.  If \(s\le2\), its positive boundary value is infinite; if
\(2<s<\sigma_0\), \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} is greater than one.
These facts give the unique interior root for \(s<\sigma_0\).  At
\(s=\sigma_0\), the boundary value is one, while it is less than one for
\(s>\sigma_0\).  This proves the asserted real-root classification.  The
triangle inequality also shows that, for \(s<\sigma_0\), the only zero of
\(1-B_s(z)\) in \(|z|\le r_s\) is the simple zero \(r_s\): equality forces
the degree-three and degree-five terms to have the same positive phase, and
\(\gcd(3,5)=1\).

Concatenation of generating words gives
\(u_{m+n}(s)\ge u_m(s)u_n(s)\).  The letters of costs \(3\) and \(5\) imply
\(u_j(s)>0\) for every \(j\ge8\).  The shifted Fekete argument used in the
proof of the strip result therefore applies without any restriction on
\(s\), and yields
\[
 \gamma_s:=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac1j\log u_j(s).
\]
If \(s<\sigma_0\), the formal identity
\(U_s=(1-B_s)^{-1}\), positivity, and the preceding root classification show
that the radius of \(U_s\) is \(r_s\).  At \(s=\sigma_0\), it is one.  If
\(s>\sigma_0\), then \(U_s(1)=(1-B_s(1))^{-1}<\infty\), while
\(u_j(s)\ge b_j(s)\) prevents the radius of \(U_s\) from exceeding
\(R_B(s)=1\).  Hence
\[
 R_U(s)=\begin{cases}r_s,&s<\sigma_0,\\1,&s\ge\sigma_0,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad \gamma_s=-\log R_U(s)\ge0.
\]
For \(H_m(s)=\sum_{j=0}^m u_j(s)\), the exact renewal identity gives
\[
 H_m(s)\le S_{-s}(m)\le4H_{m+1}(s).
\]
Since \(m^{-1}\log H_m(s)\to\gamma_s\), this proves
\eqref{eq:all-real-pressure} and the root equation.

For \(t>-\sigma_0\), put
\[
 \mu_t(p/q):=q^t e^{-P(t)c(p/q)}.
\]
The root equation makes this a probability measure.  The root lies strictly
inside the relevant convergence circle, and
\(\log q\ll d(p/q)\), so the defining series and all its parameter
derivatives converge locally uniformly.  The analytic implicit-function
theorem applies.  With \(X=\log q\) and \(C=c(p/q)\), differentiation gives
\begin{equation}
 P'(t)=\frac{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}X}{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}C},
 \qquad
 P''(t)=
 \frac{\mathbb E_{\mu_t}[(X-P'(t)C)^2]}
      {\mathbb E_{\mu_t}C}.
 \label{eq:pressure-derivatives}
\end{equation}
The fractions \(1/2\) and \(1/3\) have \((C,q)=(3,2)\) and \((5,3)\),
and \(\log2/3\ne\log3/5\).  Thus the variance in
\eqref{eq:pressure-derivatives} is strictly positive.

It remains to justify passage to the critical endpoint.  For the canonical
regular continued fraction \(p/q=[0;a_1,\ldots,a_r]\), with \(a_r>1\),
the conversion between regular and negative continued fractions gives
\[
 d(p/q)=a_1+\cdots+a_r-1,
 \qquad c(p/q)=2(a_1+\cdots+a_r)-1.
\]
The fixed-denominator mean theorem of Panov and Liehl
\cite{Panov1982,Liehl1983,AistleitnerBordaHauke2023} states that
\[
 \frac1{\phi_{\rm E}(q)}
 \sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}(a_1+\cdots+a_r)
 \sim\frac6{\pi^2}(\log q)^2.
\]
Consequently, both the numerator and denominator in
\eqref{eq:critical-pressure-slopes} converge absolutely, since they are
bounded by constant multiples of
\(\sum q^{1-\sigma_0}\log q\) and
\(\sum q^{1-\sigma_0}(\log q)^2\), respectively.  Dominated convergence in
the first identity of \eqref{eq:pressure-derivatives} gives the stated finite
positive right derivative.  The pressure is identically zero to the left of
\(t_c\), so its left derivative is zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Full large deviations across the freezing corner]
\label{thm:full-fiber-ldp}
Let \(\mathbf X_m\) be uniformly distributed on \(X_m\), and set
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 y_m(x):=\frac1m\log d_m(x),\qquad
 Y_m:=y_m(\mathbf X_m),\\
 t_c:=-\sigma_0,\qquad v_c:=P'_+(t_c),
 \qquad \alpha_+:=\frac12\log\varphi.
 \end{gathered}
\]
Then \((Y_m)\) satisfies a full large deviation principle with speed \(m\)
and good rate function
\begin{equation}
 I(\alpha)=\sup_{t\in\mathbb R}
 \{t\alpha-P(t)+\log\varphi\}.
 \label{eq:full-fiber-ldp-rate}
\end{equation}
Its effective domain is \([0,\alpha_+]\).  On the coexistence interval,
\begin{equation}
 I(\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
 \qquad(0\le\alpha\le v_c).
 \label{eq:frozen-affine-rate}
\end{equation}
More precisely, for every \(0<\alpha<v_c\),
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}
 \frac1m\log\Pr\{|Y_m-\alpha|<\varepsilon\}\\
 &\quad=
 \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\limsup_{m\to\infty}
 \frac1m\log\Pr\{|Y_m-\alpha|<\varepsilon\}\\
 &\quad=-\bigl(\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha\bigr).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:frozen-local-large-deviation}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(\alpha\in\mathbb R\) and \(\varepsilon>0\), write
\[
 A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
 :=\card{\left\{x\in X_m:
 \left|m^{-1}\log d_m(x)-\alpha\right|<\varepsilon\right\}}.
\]
We first establish the lower bound at the nonexposed slopes.  Fix
\(t>t_c\), put \(v=P'(t)>v_c\), and define the tilted law on \(X_n\) by
\[
 \nu_{n,t}(x):=\frac{d_n(x)^t}{S_t(n)}.
\]
For sufficiently small \(s>0\), the exponential Markov inequality and
\Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} give
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n
 \log\nu_{n,t}\{y_n\ge v+\delta\}
 \le P(t+s)-P(t)-s(v+\delta)<0.
\]
The corresponding estimate with \(s<0\) gives the same conclusion for
\(y_n\le v-\delta\).  Consequently,
\[
 \sum_{\substack{x\in X_n\\|y_n(x)-v|<\delta}}d_n(x)^t
 =\exp\{n(P(t)+o(1))\}.
\]
Every summand on the left is at most
\(\exp\{n(tv+|t|\delta)\}\), and hence
\begin{equation}
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log A_n(v,\delta)
 \ge P(t)-tv-|t|\delta.
 \label{eq:exposed-source-count}
\end{equation}

We now transfer these source states to a larger layer without changing their
multiplicities.  The proof of \Cref{thm:fiber-partition-interval} gives the
explicit bijection
\[
 \chi_n(x)=
 \begin{cases}
  F_{n+2}+V_n(x),&0\le V_n(x)\le F_{n+1}-2,\\
  V_n(x),&F_{n+1}-1\le V_n(x)<F_{n+2},
 \end{cases}
\]
from \(X_n\) onto the integer points of
\(\mathcal I_n\sqcup\mathcal I_{n+1}\), with
\(d_n(x)=R(\chi_n(x))\).  Take
\(\delta<v/2\), so their multiplicities exceed two for all sufficiently
large \(n\).  The uniform layer-count lemma shows that each Weinstein
generating orbit supplies at most four source states to these two layers.
The specialized formulas in the proof of
\Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal} give
\[
 L([a]g)=L(g)+2a,\qquad
 L([a]\tau g)=L(g)+1+2a,
\]
and the two sigma branches increase these lengths by one.  Hence every orbit
meeting \(\mathcal I_n\sqcup\mathcal I_{n+1}\) has generator length at most
\(n+1\).
Keeping the generating number of every orbit which occurs therefore gives a
set \(\mathcal G_n\) such that
\begin{equation}
 \card{\mathcal G_n}\ge\frac14 A_n(v,\delta),
 \qquad L(g)\le n+1\quad(g\in\mathcal G_n),
 \label{eq:source-generating-orbits}
\end{equation}
and \(n^{-1}\log R(g)\in(v-\delta,v+\delta)\), since the orbit action
preserves \(R\).  Distinct elements of
\(\mathcal G_n\) generate disjoint orbits.

Fix \(0<\alpha<v_c\), set \(\rho=\alpha/v\), and let
\(n_m=\lfloor\rho m\rfloor\).  For sufficiently large \(m\), every
\(g\in\mathcal G_{n_m}\) has \(L(g)<m\).  The generator formulas in the
proof of \Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal} permit the parity-free
definition
\[
 z_m(g):=
 \begin{cases}
 [a]g,&m-L(g)=2a,\\
 [a]\tau g,&m-L(g)=2a+1.
 \end{cases}
\]
They give \(L(z_m(g))=m\) and \(R(z_m(g))=R(g)>1\).  Thus
\(z_m(g)\in\mathcal I_m\), and \(\chi_m^{-1}\) turns these into distinct
states of \(X_m\).  Moreover,
\[
 \left|m^{-1}\log R(z_m(g))-\alpha\right|
 \le \frac{n_m}{m}\delta+\frac vm.
\]
Combining this injection with
\eqref{eq:exposed-source-count}--\eqref{eq:source-generating-orbits} yields,
for every \(\varepsilon>\rho\delta\),
\begin{equation}
 \liminf_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
 \ge \rho\bigl(P(t)-tv-|t|\delta\bigr).
 \label{eq:orbit-filling-lower-bound}
\end{equation}
First let \(\delta\downarrow0\), and then let \(t\downarrow t_c\).  Since
\(P(t)\to0\), \(P'(t)=v\to v_c\), and
\[
 \rho(P(t)-tv)=\alpha\left(\frac{P(t)}v-t\right)
 \longrightarrow-t_c\alpha=\sigma_0\alpha,
\]
we obtain
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}
 \frac1m\log A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
 \ge\sigma_0\alpha.
 \label{eq:frozen-count-lower}
\end{equation}

The matching upper bound uses the critical pressure directly.  On the set
counted by \(A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)\),
\(d_m(x)^{-\sigma_0}\ge
\exp\{-\sigma_0m(\alpha+\varepsilon)\}\).  Therefore
\[
 A_m(\alpha,\varepsilon)
 e^{-\sigma_0m(\alpha+\varepsilon)}
 \le S_{-\sigma_0}(m)=e^{o(m)},
\]
which, together with \eqref{eq:frozen-count-lower}, proves the corresponding
counting limit.  Since \(\card{X_m}=F_{m+2}\) and
\(m^{-1}\log F_{m+2}\to\log\varphi\), this is
\eqref{eq:frozen-local-large-deviation}.

We next identify the convex dual.  If \(0\le\alpha\le v_c\), then
\(P(t)=0\) for \(t\le t_c\), while convexity and the right derivative at
\(t_c\) give \(P(t)\ge v_c(t-t_c)\) for \(t\ge t_c\).  It follows that
\(t\alpha-P(t)\le t_c\alpha\) for every \(t\), with equality at
\(t=t_c\), proving \eqref{eq:frozen-affine-rate}.

The exact maximum theorem gives
\[
 \max_{x\in X_m}m^{-1}\log d_m(x)\longrightarrow\alpha_+.
\]
For \(t>0\), the bounds
\(D_m^t\le S_t(m)\le F_{m+2}D_m^t\) imply
\[
 \alpha_+\le\frac{P(t)}t
 \le\alpha_++\frac{\log\varphi}{t}.
\]
By convexity, (P') is increasing and therefore has a limit in
\((0,+\infty]\).  The identity
\[
 \frac{P(t)}t=\frac{P(0)}t+\frac1t\int_0^t P'(u)\,du
\]
and Ces\`aro convergence show that this limit is \(\alpha_+\).  Thus
\(P'(t)\uparrow\alpha_+\) as \(t\to\infty\).  Strict convexity and
analyticity on \((t_c,\infty)\) now show that every
\(\alpha\in(v_c,\alpha_+)\) is exposed by a unique \(t>t_c\).  The tilted
estimate above with \(n=m\) supplies
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}
 \frac1m\log\Pr\{|Y_m-\alpha|<\varepsilon\}
 \ge P(t)-t\alpha-\log\varphi=-I(\alpha).
 \label{eq:exposed-local-large-deviation}
\end{equation}

Finally, the logarithmic moment generating functions under the uniform law
converge on all of \(\mathbb R\) to
\[
 \Lambda(t)=P(t)-\log\varphi.
\]
The variables are eventually supported in a fixed compact interval, so they
are exponentially tight.  The G\"artner--Ellis upper bound
\cite[Theorem~2.3.6(a)]{DemboZeitouni2010} therefore gives the
large-deviation upper bound for all closed sets.

It remains to justify the effective domain and endpoint lower bounds.  If
\(\alpha<0\), letting \(t\to-\infty\) in
\eqref{eq:full-fiber-ldp-rate} and using \(P(t)=0\) gives
\(I(\alpha)=+\infty\).  If \(\alpha>\alpha_+\), the limit
\(P(t)/t\to\alpha_+\) gives the same conclusion as \(t\to\infty\).
Conversely, if \(0\le\alpha\le\alpha_+\), then
\(t\alpha-P(t)\) is bounded above on \(( -\infty,t_c]\), on the compact
interval \([t_c,0]\), and on \([0,\infty)\), where
\(P(t)\ge t\alpha_+\).  Thus the effective domain is exactly
\([0,\alpha_+]\).  The rate is a lower-semicontinuous convex function, and
its finiteness on this closed interval implies continuity relative to the
interval, including at the endpoints.

The local lower bounds already proved cover the coexistence and exposed
parts of \((0,\alpha_+)\), and points from either side approximate \(v_c\).
At \(0\), the two singleton fibers give the lower bound directly.  At
\(\alpha_+\), continuity of \(I\) and exposed points approaching from below
supply the lower bound for every open neighborhood.  Hence the lower bound
holds for every open set.  The effective domain is compact and \(I\) is
lower semicontinuous, so \(I\) is good and the full large deviation principle
follows.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Microcanonical phase separation}

The renewal description also retains the generating layer, rather than only

exec
"C:\\Windows\\System32\\WindowsPowerShell\\v1.0\\powershell.exe" -Command '$lines=Get-Content -Encoding utf8 sec_local_spectrum.tex; $lines[920..1720] -join "`n"' in D:\omega\automath\papers\publication\2026_finite_window_zeckendorf_fibers_discrete_thermodynamics_tams
 succeeded in 326ms:
The renewal description also retains the generating layer, rather than only
the multiplicity.  Let
\[
 \mathcal A:=\{p/q:0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\},
 \qquad \pi(p/q):=q^{-\sigma _0}.
\]
The critical identity \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} gives
\(\sum_{\mathcal A}\pi=1\).  Let \((C_i,H_i)_{i\ge1}\) be independent with
\[
 \Pr\{(C_i,H_i)=(c(p/q),\log q)\}=\pi(p/q),
\]
put \(S_n=\sum_{i=1}^nC_i\), \(T_n=\sum_{i=1}^nH_i\), and define the marked
renewal measure
\[
 \mathcal U:=\sum_{n\ge1}\mathcal L(S_n,T_n).
\]
The moment estimates in the proof of \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} show that
\[
 \mu_C:=\mathbb E C_1<\infty,
 \qquad \mu_H:=\mathbb E H_1<\infty,
 \qquad \frac{\mu_H}{\mu_C}=v_c.
\]

We shall use the following marked form of the already proved weighted renewal
identity.  It is recorded explicitly to retain the generating cost in the
conditional argument.  For $x\in X_m$ with \(d_m(x)>2\), let \(g_m(x)\) be
the unique generating number of the Weinstein orbit containing \(\chi_m(x)\).
Define
\[
 K_m(c):=
 \begin{cases}
  4,&c\le m-1,\\
  3,&c=m,\\
  1,&c=m+1,\\
  0,&c\ge m+2.
 \end{cases}
\]
For every Borel set \(B\subset\mathbb R\times(\log2,\infty)\),
\begin{equation}
 \card{\{x\in X_m:(L(g_m(x)),\log d_m(x))\in B\}}
 =\int_B K_m(s)e^{\sigma_0t}\,\mathcal U(ds,dt).
 \label{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}
\end{equation}
Indeed, for a generating number \(g\) of cost \(c=L(g)\), the four branches
and their layer indices are
\[
 \begin{array}{c|c}
 {}[a]g&c+2a\\
 {}[a]\tau g&c+1+2a\\
 \sigma([a]g)&c+1+2a\\
 \sigma([a]\tau g)&c+2+2a.
 \end{array}
\]
The free action makes the middle two points distinct.  Thus this orbit
contributes zero, one, or two points to \(\mathcal I_j\), according as
\(j<c\), \(j=c\), or \(j>c\).  Summing over the two disjoint layers
\(\mathcal I_m\sqcup\mathcal I_{m+1}\) gives exactly \(K_m(c)\).  The two
unit-multiplicity endpoints correspond to the empty word and are excluded by
the condition \(t>\log2\).

Under Weinstein's free-monoid bijection, a nonempty word
\(w=(p_1/q_1)\times\cdots\times(p_n/q_n)\) has
\[
 L(g)=S_n,\qquad \log R(g)=T_n,
 \qquad \pi(w)=e^{-\sigma_0T_n}.
\]
Multiplication of its renewal mass by \(e^{\sigma_0T_n}\), followed by the
orbit count \(K_m(S_n)\), therefore counts every corresponding finite-window
state once.  This proves \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}.

For the endpoint tilt, define the finite-window Gibbs measure
\[
 \widehat Q_m(x):=\frac{d_m(x)^{-\sigma_0}}{S_{-\sigma_0}(m)}
 \qquad(x\in X_m),
\]
and put \(\ell_m(x)=L(g_m(x))\) when \(d_m(x)>2\), while
\(\ell_m(x)=0\) when \(d_m(x)\le2\).

\begin{theorem}[Critical finite-size and coexistence law]
\label{thm:critical-finite-window-gibbs}
Let
\[
 \mu_C=
 \sum_{q\ge2}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}
 c(p/q)q^{-\sigma_0}.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 S_{-\sigma_0}(m)\sim\frac{4m}{\mu_C}.
 \label{eq:critical-linear-finite-size}
\end{equation}
If \(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m\) has law \(\widehat Q_m\) and
\(U\) is uniform on \([0,1]\), then
\begin{equation}
 \left(
  \frac1m\log d_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m),
  \frac1m\ell_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)
 \right)
 \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\mathrm d}(v_cU,U).
 \label{eq:critical-uniform-coexistence}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
At \(s=\sigma_0\), the coefficients \(b_j(\sigma_0)\) form a probability
distribution on the positive integers by
\eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one}.  Its span is one because its support
contains both \(3\) and \(5\), and its mean is \(\mu_C<\infty\).  The
arithmetic renewal theorem therefore gives
\[
 u_j(\sigma_0)\longrightarrow\frac1{\mu_C}.
\]
Substitution in the exact identity
\eqref{eq:weighted-window-renewal}, followed by Ces\`aro summation, proves
\eqref{eq:critical-linear-finite-size}.

We use the critical letter law \(\pi(p/q)=q^{-\sigma_0}\) and the marked
renewal variables \((S_n,T_n)\) defined above
\eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}.  For every bounded continuous
function \(f\) on \([0,1]\), the arithmetic renewal limit and a Riemann-sum
argument give
\[
 \frac1m\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(\sigma_0)f(j/m)
 \longrightarrow\frac1{\mu_C}\int_0^1 f(y)\,dy.
\]
The terms at \(j=m,m+1\) are bounded, while
\(K_m(j)=4\) for \(j\le m-1\).  The total critical weight of the states
with multiplicity at most two is also bounded uniformly in \(m\).
Multiplying \eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count} by
\(e^{-\sigma_0t}\), and then using
\eqref{eq:critical-linear-finite-size}, proves
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\ell_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)}m
 \xrightarrow{\mathrm d}U.
 \label{eq:critical-uniform-layer}
\end{equation}

It remains to retain the reward mark.  The strong law gives
\(T_n/S_n\to\mu_H/\mu_C=v_c\) almost surely.  For \(\delta>0\), let
\[
 A_m=\mathcal U\{0<S\le m+1,\ |T/S-v_c|>\delta\}.
\]
Every renewal step has cost at least three.  Hence, on each sample path, the
number of renewal epochs with \(S_n\le m+1\), divided by \(m\), is bounded;
the corresponding number of exceptional epochs divided by \(m\) tends to
zero by the strong law.  Dominated convergence yields \(A_m=o(m)\).
Since \(K_m\le4\), the marked identity and
\eqref{eq:critical-linear-finite-size} now imply
\[
 \frac1m\log d_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)
 -v_c\frac1m\ell_m(\widehat{\mathbf X}_m)
 \xrightarrow{\Pr}0.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:critical-uniform-layer}, this proves
\eqref{eq:critical-uniform-coexistence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Finite-prime-support coefficient interface]
\label{prop:prime-support-interface}
Fix distinct primes \(\mathcal P=\{p_1,\ldots,p_d\}\).  For
\(\mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_d)\in\mathbb N^d\setminus\{\mathbf0\}\), put
\[
 q_{\mathbf a}:=\prod_{i=1}^d p_i^{a_i},
 \qquad
 A_{\mathbf a}(u):=
 \sum_{\substack{1\le h<q_{\mathbf a}\\(h,q_{\mathbf a})=1}}
 u^{c(h/q_{\mathbf a})},
\]
and define the formal series
\[
 A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,u)
 :=\sum_{\mathbf a\ne\mathbf0}
 A_{\mathbf a}(u)\mathbf z^{\mathbf a}.
\]
If
\[
 U_j(q_{\mathbf n})
 :=\card{\{g:\text{\(g\) is a Weinstein generator, }
 R(g)=q_{\mathbf n},\ L(g)=j\}},
\]
with the empty-word convention \(U_0(1)=1\), then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{\mathbf n\in\mathbb N^d}\sum_{j\ge0}
 U_j(q_{\mathbf n})\mathbf z^{\mathbf n}u^j
 =\frac1{1-A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,u)}.
 \label{eq:prime-support-joint-renewal}
\end{equation}
For \(q_{\mathbf n}>2\), the exact finite-window count is
\begin{equation}
 C_m(q_{\mathbf n})
 =\sum_{j\ge0}K_m(j)U_j(q_{\mathbf n}).
 \label{eq:prime-support-window-interface}
\end{equation}
After the cost mark is removed, the series is rational:
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{\mathbf n\in\mathbb N^d}
 \left(\sum_{j\ge0}U_j(q_{\mathbf n})\right)\mathbf z^{\mathbf n}
 =\frac1{\displaystyle
 2-\prod_{i=1}^d\frac{1-z_i}{1-p_i z_i}}.
 \label{eq:prime-support-unmarked-rational}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Under Weinstein's free-monoid parameterization, a letter with denominator
\(q_{\mathbf a}\) contributes exponent vector \(\mathbf a\), cost
\(c(h/q_{\mathbf a})\), and multiplier \(q_{\mathbf a}\).  Unique ordered
factorization into letters gives \eqref{eq:prime-support-joint-renewal} by the
formal sequence construction.  The four orbit branches counted in
\eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count} depend only on the generator cost and
give \eqref{eq:prime-support-window-interface}.  Finally,
\[
 \sum_{a\ge0}\phi_{\rm E}(p_i^a)z_i^a
 =\frac{1-z_i}{1-p_i z_i}.
\]
Multiplying over the primes shows that
\(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)\) is the displayed product minus one, which
proves \eqref{eq:prime-support-unmarked-rational}.
\end{proof}

Write
\[
 \mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}:=
 \{\boldsymbol\rho\in(0,\infty)^d:
   \rho_1+\cdots+\rho_d=1\}.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Directional quenched law on finite prime support]
\label{thm:prime-support-directional-quenched}
Retain the notation of \Cref{prop:prime-support-interface}, and fix
\(\boldsymbol\rho\in\mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}\).  Put
\[
 g_i(z):=\frac{1-z}{1-p_i z},
 \qquad
 h_i(z):=\frac{z g_i'(z)}{g_i(z)}
 =\frac{(p_i-1)z}{(1-z)(1-p_i z)}.
\]
There are unique \(\tau>0\) and
\(\mathbf z=\mathbf z(\boldsymbol\rho)\in
\prod_i(0,p_i^{-1})\) such that
\begin{equation}
 \prod_{i=1}^d g_i(z_i)=2,
 \qquad h_i(z_i)=\tau\rho_i\quad(1\le i\le d).
 \label{eq:prime-support-directional-saddle}
\end{equation}
More explicitly, \(z_i=\zeta_i(\tau\rho_i)\), where
\begin{equation}
 \zeta_i(x)=
 \frac{(p_i-1)+(p_i+1)x-
  \sqrt{((p_i-1)+(p_i+1)x)^2-4p_i x^2}}
 {2p_i x}.
 \label{eq:prime-support-correct-inverse}
\end{equation}
The point \(\mathbf z\) is the unique point of
\(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf w,1)=1\) in the closed polydisc
\(\{|w_i|\le z_i\}\).

Give every letter \((\mathbf a,h/q_{\mathbf a})\) the probability
\begin{equation}
 \Pr_{\mathbf z}\{(\mathbf A,C)=
  (\mathbf a,c(h/q_{\mathbf a}))\}=\mathbf z^{\mathbf a}.
 \label{eq:prime-support-directional-letter-law}
\end{equation}
This is a probability law, and
\[
 \mathbb E_{\mathbf z}\mathbf A=2\tau\boldsymbol\rho,
 \qquad m_C(\boldsymbol\rho):=\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}C<\infty.
\]
Define its directional velocity by
\begin{equation}
 v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}:=
 \frac{2\tau\sum_i\rho_i\log p_i}{m_C(\boldsymbol\rho)}.
 \label{eq:prime-support-directional-velocity}
\end{equation}

Let \(\mathbf n_N=N\boldsymbol\rho+O(1)\in\mathbb N^d\), and let
\(W_N\) be uniform among the nonempty free generator words of total exponent
\(\mathbf n_N\).  If \(R_N\) is its number of letters and \(T_N\) its
total cost, then
\begin{equation}
 \frac{R_N}{N}\xrightarrow{\Pr}\frac1{2\tau},
 \qquad
 \frac{T_N}{N}\xrightarrow{\Pr}\frac{m_C(\boldsymbol\rho)}{2\tau},
 \qquad
 \frac{\log q_{\mathbf n_N}}{T_N}
 \xrightarrow{\Pr}v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}.
 \label{eq:prime-support-directional-free-law}
\end{equation}

Finally, let \(m_N\to\infty\) be integers such that
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\log q_{\mathbf n_N}}{m_N}\longrightarrow
 \alpha\in[0,v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}).
 \label{eq:prime-support-inactive-budget}
\end{equation}
Then the exact level
\(\{x\in X_{m_N}:d_{m_N}(x)=q_{\mathbf n_N}\}\) is nonempty for all
sufficiently large \(N\),
\begin{equation}
 C_{m_N}(q_{\mathbf n_N})
 \sim4\card{\{\text{free words of exponent }\mathbf n_N\}},
 \label{eq:prime-support-inactive-count}
\end{equation}
and, under the uniform law on this level,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{L(g_{m_N})}{m_N}
 \xrightarrow{\Pr}
 \frac{\alpha}{v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}}.
 \label{eq:prime-support-inactive-window-law}
\end{equation}
The free-word convergences are uniform when \(\boldsymbol\rho\) ranges over a
compact subset of \(\mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}\) and
\(\|\mathbf n_N-N\boldsymbol\rho\|\) is bounded.  The finite-window
conclusions are uniform under the additional requirement that the budget
ratio remain bounded away from one.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Each \(h_i\) is strictly increasing from zero to infinity on
\((0,p_i^{-1})\).  Solving the resulting quadratic gives
\eqref{eq:prime-support-correct-inverse}.  Hence
\(\prod_i g_i(\zeta_i(\tau\rho_i))\) increases continuously from one to
infinity, which proves existence and uniqueness in
\eqref{eq:prime-support-directional-saddle}.  Since
\(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)=1\),
\eqref{eq:prime-support-directional-letter-law} has total mass one, and
Euler differentiation gives
\[
 \mathbb E_{\mathbf z}A_i
 =z_i\partial_{z_i}A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)
 =2h_i(z_i)=2\tau\rho_i.
\]

We verify the strict-minimality and lattice hypotheses needed below.  If
\(|w_i|\le z_i\) and \(A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf w,1)=1\), coefficient
positivity gives
\[
 1\le A_{\mathcal P}(|\mathbf w|,1)
 \le A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf z,1)=1.
\]
The support contains every \(\mathbf e_i\), so equality in the second
inequality forces \(|w_i|=z_i\) for every \(i\).  Equality in the triangle
inequality then forces all supported monomials to have phase one; applying
this to the \(\mathbf e_i\) gives \(w_i=z_i\).  The same support contains
\(2\mathbf e_i\), so its difference lattice is \(\mathbb Z^d\).  Finally,
the covariance matrix of \(\mathbf A\) is positive definite: if
\(\mathbf t\cdot\mathbf A\) were constant almost surely, comparison of
\(\mathbf e_i\) and \(2\mathbf e_i\) would give \(t_i=0\) for every
\(i\).

The fixed-denominator mean estimate quoted in the proof of
\Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} gives
\[
 \sum_{\substack{1\le h<q\\(h,q)=1}}c(h/q)
 =O(q(1+\log q)^2).
\]
Since \(p_i z_i<1\), summation over exponent vectors proves
\(m_C(\boldsymbol\rho)<\infty\).

We now apply the smooth strictly minimal point expansion of
Pemantle--Wilson \cite{PemantleWilson2002}; the preceding paragraph verifies
its nonperiodicity, strict minimality, and nondegenerate transverse Hessian
for the present rational function.  Marking each letter by \(e^s\) and
using the expansion uniformly for small complex \(s\) gives the usual
analytic quasi-powers estimate.  The critical number of letters is determined
by
\[
 r_N\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}\mathbf A=N\boldsymbol\rho,
 \qquad\text{hence}\qquad r_N=\frac{N}{2\tau}.
\]
It follows that \(R_N/N\to1/(2\tau)\) in probability.

For \(M>0\), mark instead the bounded cost \(C\wedge M\).  The same analytic
perturbation gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac1N\sum_{j=1}^{R_N}(C_j\wedge M)
 \xrightarrow{\Pr}\frac{\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}(C\wedge M)}{2\tau}.
 \label{eq:prime-support-truncated-cost-law}
\end{equation}
To remove the truncation, put
\[
 D_M(\mathbf x):=
 \sum_{\text{letters }\ell}C(\ell)\mathbf1_{\{C(\ell)>M\}}
 \mathbf x^{\mathbf A(\ell)}.
\]
This series is analytic in a neighborhood of \(\mathbf z\).  Pointing one
letter in a free word gives the exact coefficient identity
\[
 \mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{R_N}C_j\mathbf1_{\{C_j>M\}}
 =\frac{[\mathbf x^{\mathbf n_N}]
  D_M(\mathbf x)(1-A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf x,1))^{-2}}
 {[\mathbf x^{\mathbf n_N}]
  (1-A_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf x,1))^{-1}}.
\]
The corresponding smooth-point expansions, or equivalently differentiation
of the analytic perturbation
\(A_{\mathcal P}+sD_M\) at \(s=0\), yield
\[
 \frac1N\mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^{R_N}
 C_j\mathbf1_{\{C_j>M\}}
 \longrightarrow
 \frac{\mathbb E_{\mathbf z}[C\mathbf1_{\{C>M\}}]}{2\tau}.
\]
First let \(N\to\infty\), then \(M\to\infty\), and use Markov's
inequality together with \eqref{eq:prime-support-truncated-cost-law}.  This
proves the cost limit in \eqref{eq:prime-support-directional-free-law}; the
velocity limit follows from
\(N^{-1}\log q_{\mathbf n_N}\to\sum_i\rho_i\log p_i\).

Under \eqref{eq:prime-support-inactive-budget}, the free law gives
\[
 \frac{T_N}{m_N}\xrightarrow{\Pr}
 \frac{\alpha}{v_{\mathcal P,\boldsymbol\rho}}<1.
\]
Thus \(\Pr\{T_N\le m_N-1\}\to1\).  By
\eqref{eq:prime-support-window-interface}, the finite-window law on generator
words is obtained from the uniform free-word law by the weight
\(K_{m_N}(T_N)\).  Since this weight is bounded by four and equals four on
an event of probability tending to one, its normalizing constant tends to
four and the two laws converge in total variation.  This proves
\eqref{eq:prime-support-inactive-count} and
\eqref{eq:prime-support-inactive-window-law}.

On compact subsets of \(\mathbb S_{+}^{d-1}\), the saddle remains in a
compact subset of \(\prod_i(0,p_i^{-1})\), the transverse covariance stays
uniformly nondegenerate, and the fixed-denominator bound supplies a common
summable majorant for the cost tails.  The smooth-point expansions and all
truncation estimates above are therefore uniform there.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Located active-cutoff obstruction]
\label{rem:prime-support-local-open-interface}
Equations \eqref{eq:prime-support-joint-renewal} and
\eqref{eq:prime-support-window-interface} locate, but do not solve, the
active arithmetic interface.  The unmarked rational function is in the scope
of smooth-point multivariate coefficient methods such as
Pemantle--Wilson \cite{PemantleWilson2002}; the nearest ordered-factorization
comparators are Lau \cite{Lau2001} and Hwang--Janson
\cite{HwangJanson2011}.  Those results do not retain the continued-fraction
cost at an exponent-conditioned lattice point.  The inactive regime is
covered by \Cref{thm:prime-support-directional-quenched}; the issue below
begins when the cutoff meets the typical directional cost.

Already for \(\mathcal P=\{2\}\), the normalized letter law used in the
dyadic renewal assigns mass \(3^{-a}\) to each fraction with denominator
\(2^a\).  Since
\(c(1/2^a)=2^{a+1}-1\), its cost \(C\) satisfies
\[
 \mathbb E C^2
 \ge\sum_{a\ge1}3^{-a}(2^{a+1}-1)^2=+\infty,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E e^{\theta C}=+\infty\quad(\theta>0).
\]
Its first moment is finite: the Panov--Liehl mean estimate used above makes
the contribution at exponent \(a\) at most
\(O(a^2(2/3)^a)\).  Thus neither a Gaussian local theorem nor a two-sided
Cram\'er tilt is available at \(u=1\).  A sharp asymptotic for
\(C_m(q_{\mathbf n})\) with the cutoff \(j=m+O(1)\) requires a lattice
stable or semistable local-renewal theorem uniform under multivariate exponent
conditioning, followed by the nonsmooth weights \(K_m(m)=3\) and
\(K_m(m+1)=1\).  No such theorem is proved or invoked here; this is the exact
remaining open input.
\end{remark}

\fi
\ifdefined\supplementonly
\begin{theorem}[Finite-window conditional phase separation]
\label{thm:conditional-phase-separation}
Fix \(0<\alpha<v_c\) and
\[
 0<\varepsilon<\min\{\alpha,v_c-\alpha\}.
\]
Let \(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}\) be uniform on
\[
 \left\{x\in X_m:
 \left|m^{-1}\log d_m(x)-\alpha\right|<\varepsilon\right\}.
\]
This set is nonempty for all sufficiently large \(m\), and
\begin{equation}
 \left(
  \frac1m\log d_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}),
  \frac1mL(g_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}))
 \right)
 \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\Pr}
 \left(\alpha+\varepsilon,
       \frac{\alpha+\varepsilon}{v_c}\right).
 \label{eq:conditional-phase-separation}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every \(\eta>0\),
\[
 \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\lim_{m\to\infty}
 \Pr\left\{
  \left|m^{-1}L(g_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon}))
       -\frac\alpha{v_c}\right|>\eta
 \right\}=0.
\]
For fixed \(\varepsilon\), the outer limit is essential: if
\(0<\eta<\varepsilon/v_c\), then the probability in the last display tends
to one.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put \(a=\alpha-\varepsilon\), \(b=\alpha+\varepsilon\), and
\(r=\mu_C/\mu_H=1/v_c\).  We first record the two renewal facts used below.
For each fixed \(h>0\), Blackwell's nonarithmetic renewal theorem
\cite[Ch.~XI]{Feller1971} gives
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal U\{t<T\le t+h\}\longrightarrow\frac h{\mu_H}.
 \label{eq:blackwell-critical-renewal}
\end{equation}
The law of \(H_1\) is nonarithmetic because it charges both \(\log2\) and
\(\log3\), whose ratio is irrational.  The strong law gives
\(S_n/T_n\to r\) almost surely.  Moreover, since \(H_i\ge\log2\), an interval
of length \(h\) contains at most
\(N_h:=\lfloor h/\log2\rfloor+1\) renewal epochs on every sample path.
Dominated convergence therefore yields, for each \(\delta>0\),
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal U\{t<T\le t+h,\ |S/T-r|>\delta\}\longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:marked-renewal-reward-law}
\end{equation}

Choose \(\delta_0>0\) so that \((r+\delta_0)b<1\), and fix \(h>0\).
On
\[
 J_m=(bm-2h,bm-h]
\]
the part of \(\mathcal U\) satisfying \(|S/T-r|\le\delta_0\) has mass
\(h/\mu_H+o(1)\) by
\eqref{eq:blackwell-critical-renewal}--\eqref{eq:marked-renewal-reward-law}.
For all sufficiently large \(m\), every such point has \(S\le m-1\), and
hence \(K_m(S)=4\).  If \(Z_m\) denotes the cardinality of the conditioning
set, \eqref{main-eq:marked-microcanonical-count} gives
\begin{equation}
 Z_m\ge4e^{\sigma_0(bm-2h)}
       \left(\frac h{\mu_H}+o(1)\right).
 \label{eq:conditional-denominator-lower}
\end{equation}

For fixed \(\delta>0\), set
\[
 \rho_m:=\sup_{t\ge am-h}
 \mathcal U\{t<T\le t+h,\ |S/T-r|>\delta\}.
\]
Equation \eqref{eq:marked-renewal-reward-law} implies \(\rho_m\to0\).
Covering \((am,bm)\) from its upper endpoint by intervals of length \(h\),
and using \(K_m\le4\), bounds the number of exceptional states by
\[
 \frac{4e^{\sigma_0bm}}{1-e^{-\sigma_0h}}\rho_m.
\]
After division by \eqref{eq:conditional-denominator-lower}, this proves
\[
 \frac{L(g_m)}{\log d_m}=\frac ST\xrightarrow{\Pr}r
\]
under the conditional law.

Finally, \(\mathcal U\{t<T\le t+h\}\le N_h\) for every \(t\).  Thus, for
each \(\gamma>0\), the number of conditioned states with
\(T\le(b-\gamma)m\) is
\(O(me^{\sigma_0(b-\gamma)m})\).  Equation
\eqref{eq:conditional-denominator-lower} shows that their conditional
probability tends to zero.  Since the conditioning itself gives
\(T/m<b\), we have \(T/m\to b\) in probability.  Combining this with
\(S/T\to r\) proves \eqref{eq:conditional-phase-separation}; its stated
consequences follow immediately.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Admissible shrinking windows]
\label{cor:shrinking-window-phase-separation}
Under the hypotheses of \Cref{thm:conditional-phase-separation}, suppose that
\(\varepsilon_m\downarrow0\) and
\(\liminf_{m\to\infty}m\varepsilon_m>0\).  Then the conditioning sets are
nonempty for all sufficiently large \(m\), and
\[
 \frac1mL(g_m(\mathbf X_{m,\alpha,\varepsilon_m}))
 \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\Pr}\frac\alpha{v_c}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Choose a fixed \(h>0\) smaller than
\(\liminf m\varepsilon_m\).  The interval
\(((\alpha+\varepsilon_m)m-2h,
   (\alpha+\varepsilon_m)m-h]\) then lies inside the conditioning window for
all sufficiently large \(m\).  The proof of
\Cref{thm:conditional-phase-separation}, with this fixed interval, gives the
same denominator lower bound and the same uniform estimate
\(S/T\to1/v_c\).  The conditioning itself now gives \(T/m\to\alpha\), which
proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Arithmetic obstruction for thinner windows]
\label{rem:shrinking-window-arithmetic-obstruction}
The preceding corollary cannot be extended to every sequence
\(\varepsilon_m\downarrow0\).  Put
\(\mathcal D_m=\{\log d_m(x):x\in X_m\}\).  For every
\(0<\alpha<v_c\), there are infinitely many \(m\) for which
\(\alpha m\notin\mathcal D_m\).  Indeed, otherwise \(e^{\alpha m}\) and
\(e^{\alpha(m+1)}\) would be integers for all large \(m\), forcing
\(e^\alpha\) to be an integer; this contradicts
\(0<\alpha<v_c\le\alpha_+=\tfrac12\log\varphi<\log2\).
Enumerate such a subsequence as \((m_j)\).  Starting from any
\(\varepsilon_1>0\), define the remaining widths recursively so that
\(0<\varepsilon_m<\varepsilon_{m-1}/2\), and, when \(m=m_j\), impose also
\[
 \varepsilon_m<\frac1m
 \operatorname{dist}(\alpha m,\mathcal D_m).
\]
This gives a decreasing sequence for which the conditioning set is empty at
every \(m_j\).  Thus windows on a smaller scale require an additional
arithmetic local theorem, not merely a uniform version of Blackwell renewal.
\end{remark}

The preceding obstruction concerns arbitrary thin windows.  A complete
arithmetic conditional limit can nevertheless be obtained along the dyadic
multiplier ray.  For \(r\ge1\), let
\[
 \mathcal G_r:=\{g:\text{\(g\) is a Weinstein generator and }R(g)=2^r\}.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Exact dyadic multiplier limit]
\label{thm:dyadic-exact-multiplier}
The series
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_2:=\frac13\sum_{a\ge1}3^{-a}
 \sum_{\substack{1\le p<2^a\\p\ \mathrm{odd}}}c(p/2^a)
 \label{eq:dyadic-cost-velocity}
\end{equation}
converges.  Moreover,
\[
 \card{\mathcal G_r}=3^{r-1}\qquad(r\ge1).
\]
If \(G_r\) is uniform on \(\mathcal G_r\), then
\begin{equation}
 \frac{L(G_r)}r\xrightarrow[r\to\infty]{\Pr}\gamma_2.
 \label{eq:dyadic-generator-bridge}
\end{equation}

Let \(r_m\to\infty\) be integers such that
\[
 \frac m{r_m}\longrightarrow\beta>\gamma_2.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 C_m(2^{r_m})\sim4\cdot3^{r_m-1}.
 \label{eq:dyadic-finite-window-count}
\end{equation}
In particular, the exact level \(\{x\in X_m:d_m(x)=2^{r_m}\}\) is nonempty
for all sufficiently large \(m\).  If \(\mathbf X_m^{(2)}\) is uniform on
this level, then
\begin{equation}
 \frac{L(g_m(\mathbf X_m^{(2)}))}{r_m}
 \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\Pr}\gamma_2,
 \qquad
 \frac{L(g_m(\mathbf X_m^{(2)}))}{m}
 \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\Pr}\frac{\gamma_2}{\beta}.
 \label{eq:dyadic-finite-window-bridge}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, with \(v_2=\log2/\gamma_2\), if
\(r_m=\lfloor\alpha m/\log2\rfloor\) and \(0<\alpha<v_2\), the last limit is
\(\alpha/v_2\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If a free generator word has multiplier \(2^r\), every denominator in the
word is a power of \(2\).  For each \(a\ge1\), there are
\(\phi_{\rm E}(2^a)=2^{a-1}\) letters with denominator \(2^a\).  Hence the
ordinary generating function for such words, including the empty word, is
\[
 \frac1{1-\sum_{a\ge1}2^{a-1}z^a}
 =\frac{1-2z}{1-3z}.
\]
Coefficient extraction proves \(\card{\mathcal G_r}=3^{r-1}\) for \(r\ge1\).

Define an independent letter sequence \((A_i,C_i)_{i\ge1}\) by
\[
 \Pr\{(A_i,C_i)=(a,c(p/2^a))\}=3^{-a}
 \quad(a\ge1,\ 1\le p<2^a,\ p\ \mathrm{odd}).
\]
This is a probability distribution because
\[
 \sum_{a\ge1}2^{a-1}3^{-a}=1,
 \qquad \mathbb E A_1=3.
\]
For the canonical regular continued fraction of \(p/2^a\), the
Panov--Liehl fixed-denominator mean theorem
\cite{Panov1982,Liehl1983,AistleitnerBordaHauke2023} gives, as \(a\to\infty\),
\[
 2^{1-a}\sum_{\substack{1\le p<2^a\\p\ \mathrm{odd}}}
 c(p/2^a)=O(a^2).
\]
Consequently \(\mathbb E C_1<\infty\), and
\(\mathbb E C_1/\mathbb E A_1=\gamma_2\).

Put \(A^{(n)}=\sum_{i=1}^nA_i\) and
\(C^{(n)}=\sum_{i=1}^nC_i\).  Every word with total exponent \(r\) has
probability \(3^{-r}\).  Therefore
\[
 \Pr\{A^{(n)}=r\text{ for some }n\}
 =3^{-r}\card{\mathcal G_r}=\frac13.
\]
Conditioned on this renewal event, the word is uniform on \(\mathcal G_r\).
The strong law gives
\(C^{(n)}/A^{(n)}\to\mathbb E C_1/\mathbb E A_1=\gamma_2\) almost surely.
On every sample path, the indicator that a renewal visit to \(r\) violates a
fixed neighborhood of this limit therefore tends to zero as \(r\to\infty\).
Dominated convergence, followed by division by the constant hitting
probability \(1/3\), proves \eqref{eq:dyadic-generator-bridge}.

For \(j\ge0\), let
\[
 U_j(2^r):=\card{\{g\in\mathcal G_r:L(g)=j\}}.
\]
The four-branch formula \eqref{main-eq:marked-microcanonical-count} gives, for
\(r\ge2\),
\begin{equation}
 C_m(2^r)=\sum_{j\ge0}K_m(j)U_j(2^r).
 \label{eq:dyadic-four-branch-count}
\end{equation}
Under the hypotheses on \(r_m\),
\eqref{eq:dyadic-generator-bridge} implies
\[
 \Pr\{L(G_{r_m})\le m-1\}\longrightarrow1,
\]
because \(m/r_m\to\beta>\gamma_2\).  Since \(0\le K_m\le4\) and
\(K_m(j)=4\) for \(j\le m-1\), division of
\eqref{eq:dyadic-four-branch-count} by \(4\card{\mathcal G_{r_m}}\) proves
\eqref{eq:dyadic-finite-window-count}.  The cost law under the uniform exact
fiber level is obtained from the uniform law on \(\mathcal G_{r_m}\) by the
bounded weight \(K_m/4\), which converges to \(1\) in probability and in
\(L^1\).  Thus the two laws have total-variation distance tending to zero.
Equation \eqref{eq:dyadic-generator-bridge} now gives
\eqref{eq:dyadic-finite-window-bridge}.  The final formulation follows from
\(r_m/m\to\alpha/\log2\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The theorem identifies the quenched speed on one arithmetic multiplier ray.
The dyadic exponent--cost lattice has no proper sublattice obstruction:
the two letter vectors \((1,3)\) and \((2,7)\) have determinant \(1\).
The comparison with the critical annealed velocity requires the explicit
certificate given next.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Strict dyadic--critical speed separation]
\label{thm:strict-speed-separation}
The dyadic velocity in \Cref{thm:dyadic-exact-multiplier} and the critical
velocity in \Cref{main-thm:full-fiber-ldp} satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
  5.185556312796440793\le\gamma_2
  \le5.235362589164389880,\\
  v_2\ge0.132397168057576261,
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:dyadic-speed-certificate}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 v_c\le0.131225207544812100.
 \label{eq:critical-speed-certificate}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 v_2-v_c\ge0.001171960512764161>0.
 \label{eq:strict-speed-separation}
\end{equation}

In particular, fix \(0<\alpha<v_c\), put
\(r_m=\lfloor\alpha m/\log2\rfloor\), and condition uniformly on the exact
level \(d_m=2^{r_m}\).  This level is nonempty for all sufficiently large
\(m\), and
\begin{equation}
 \frac{L(g_m)}m\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\Pr}
 \frac{\alpha}{v_2}\ne\frac{\alpha}{v_c}.
 \label{eq:dyadic-uniform-speed-counterexample}
\end{equation}
Thus the critical conditional speed does not extend uniformly from bands to
all realizable exact multiplier sequences.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a reduced fraction \(p/q\in(0,1)\), let \(S(p/q)\) denote the sum of the
partial quotients in its canonical regular continued fraction, so that
\(c(p/q)=2S(p/q)-1\).  Set
\[
 \begin{gathered}
  T(q):=\sum_{\substack{1\le p<q\\(p,q)=1}}S(p/q),
  \qquad
  \mathcal C(q):=2T(q)-\phi_{\rm E}(q),\\
  A(q):=\sum_{n=1}^{q-1}\tau(n)\tau(q-n).
 \end{gathered}
\]
We first establish an explicit bound for \(\mathcal C(q)\).  Each of the
\(S(p/q)-1\) strict ancestor intervals of \(p/q\) in the Stern--Brocot tree
has ordered adjacent endpoints, say \(a/b<c/d\), and there are unique
positive integers \(u,v\) such that
\[
 \frac pq=\frac{ua+vc}{ub+vd}.
\]
Ordered coprime denominators \(b,d\) determine their Farey adjacent interval
uniquely.  Hence the map from a fraction together with one of its strict
ancestors to \((b,u,d,v)\) is injective, and

 succeeded in 162ms:
\section{Thermodynamics of the Fibonacci partition function}
\label{sec:standard-thermodynamics}

The renewal identity was derived from Weinstein's standard Fibonacci
partition function before it was transferred to the finite-window fold.  We
now state its one-layer consequences intrinsically.  This removes the bespoke
fold from the probabilistic conclusions.

For \(m\ge1\), recall
\[
 \mathcal I_m=[F_{m+1}-1,F_{m+2}-1)
\]
and define
\[
 Z_m^R(t):=\sum_{n\in\mathcal I_m}R(n)^t
 \qquad(t\in\RR).
\]
If \(\mathbf N_m\) is uniform on \(\mathcal I_m\), put
\[
 Y_m^R:=\frac1m\log R(\mathbf N_m).
\]

\begin{theorem}[One-layer Fibonacci partition thermodynamics]
\label{thm:standard-fibonacci-thermodynamics}
For every \(t\in\RR\),
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log Z_m^R(t)=P(t),
 \label{eq:standard-layer-pressure}
\end{equation}
where \(P\) is the pressure in \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure}.  Thus the
one-layer pressure is zero on \((-\infty,-\sigma_0]\), is positive, real
analytic, and strictly convex on \((-\sigma_0,\infty)\), and has the
one-sided derivatives in \eqref{eq:critical-pressure-slopes}.

For \(s>\sigma_0\), the unnormalized sums satisfy
\begin{equation}
 Z_m^R(-s)\longrightarrow
 \mathcal Z_R(s):=
 \frac2{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}-1.
 \label{eq:standard-layer-frozen-limit}
\end{equation}
At the critical tilt,
\begin{equation}
 Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)\sim\frac{2m}{\mu_C}.
 \label{eq:standard-layer-critical-size}
\end{equation}

For \(n\in\mathcal I_m\) with \(R(n)>2\), let \(g(n)\) be the generating
number of its Weinstein orbit and put \(\ell_m^R(n)=L(g(n))\); set
\(\ell_m^R(n)=0\) when \(R(n)\le2\).  Let \(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m\) have the
critical Gibbs law
\[
 \Pr\{\widehat{\mathbf N}_m=n\}
 =\frac{R(n)^{-\sigma_0}}{Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)}
 \qquad(n\in\mathcal I_m).
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 \left(
  \frac1m\log R(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m),
  \frac1m\ell_m^R(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m)
 \right)
 \xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{\mathrm d}(v_cU,U),
 \label{eq:standard-layer-critical-gibbs}
\end{equation}
where \(U\) is uniform on \([0,1]\).

Finally, \((Y_m^R)\) satisfies a full large deviation principle with speed
\(m\) and the good rate function
\begin{equation}
 I(\alpha)=\sup_{t\in\RR}
 \{t\alpha-P(t)+\log\varphi\}.
 \label{eq:standard-layer-ldp-rate}
\end{equation}
Its effective domain is \([0,\alpha_+]\), where
\(\alpha_+=\tfrac12\log\varphi\), and
\[
 I(\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
 \qquad(0\le\alpha\le v_c).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put \(H_m(s)=\sum_{j=0}^m u_j(s)\).  The exact one-layer renewal identity
\eqref{eq:weighted-layer-renewal} says
\begin{equation}
 Z_m^R(-s)=2H_{m-1}(s)+u_m(s)-1.
 \label{eq:standard-layer-renewal}
\end{equation}
The proof of \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} established
\[
 \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log u_m(s)
 =\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log H_m(s)
 =P(-s).
\]
Since \(u_m(s)\le Z_m^R(-s)\le3H_m(s)\), this proves
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-pressure}.

If \(s>\sigma_0\), then \(U_s(1)=\sum_{j\ge0}u_j(s)<\infty\), so
\(u_m(s)\to0\).  Equations
\eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function} and
\eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} give
\[
 U_s(1)=\frac1{1-B_s(1)}
 =\frac1{2-\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)}.
\]
Letting \(m\to\infty\) in \eqref{eq:standard-layer-renewal} proves
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-frozen-limit}.  At \(s=\sigma_0\), the arithmetic
renewal theorem used in \Cref{thm:critical-finite-window-gibbs} gives
\(u_m(\sigma_0)\to1/\mu_C\).  Ces\`aro summation in
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-renewal} proves
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-size}.

We next retain the generating cost.  The four orbit branches displayed above
\eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count} show that a generator of cost \(c\)
contributes two points to \(\mathcal I_m\) if \(c<m\), one if \(c=m\), and
none if \(c>m\).  Thus the one-layer boundary weight is
\[
 K_m^R(c)=
 \begin{cases}
  2,&c<m,\\
  1,&c=m,\\
  0,&c>m.
 \end{cases}
\]
With the marked renewal measure \(\mathcal U\) defined before
\eqref{eq:marked-microcanonical-count}, the same free-monoid bijection gives,
for every Borel set
\(B\subset\mathbb R\times(\log2,\infty)\),
\begin{equation}
 \card{\{n\in\mathcal I_m:
   (\ell_m^R(n),\log R(n))\in B\}}
 =\int_B K_m^R(c)e^{\sigma_0h}\,\mathcal U(dc,dh).
 \label{eq:standard-layer-marked-count}
\end{equation}
The points with \(R(n)\le2\) have bounded total critical weight, uniformly in
\(m\), by the uniform layer-count lemma.

For every bounded continuous \(f\) on \([0,1]\), the arithmetic renewal
theorem and a Riemann-sum argument give
\[
 \frac1m\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(\sigma_0)f(j/m)
 \longrightarrow\frac1{\mu_C}\int_0^1f(y)\,dy.
\]
After multiplication of \eqref{eq:standard-layer-marked-count} by
\(e^{-\sigma_0h}\), the weights are two for \(j<m\) and one at \(j=m\).
Together with \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-size}, this proves
\[
 \frac{\ell_m^R(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m)}m
 \xrightarrow{\mathrm d}U.
\]
For the reward coordinate, the critical renewal increments
\((C_i,H_i)\) satisfy
\[
 \frac{H_1+\cdots+H_r}{C_1+\cdots+C_r}
 \longrightarrow\frac{\mu_H}{\mu_C}=v_c
\quad\text{almost surely}.
\]
Every cost increment is at least three.  Consequently the number of renewal
epochs with total cost at most \(m\) and reward-to-cost ratio outside a fixed
\(\delta\)-neighborhood of \(v_c\) is \(o(m)\), pathwise and, by bounded
convergence after division by \(m\), in expectation.  Since \(K_m^R\le2\),
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-marked-count} and
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-size} imply
\[
 \frac1m\log R(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m)
 -v_c\frac{\ell_m^R(\widehat{\mathbf N}_m)}m
 \xrightarrow{\Pr}0.
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-gibbs}.

It remains to prove the full LDP, in particular the lower bound at the
nonexposed slopes.  The layer has
\(\card{\mathcal I_m}=F_m\), and hence
\[
 \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log\card{\mathcal I_m}=\log\varphi.
\]
Equation \eqref{eq:standard-layer-pressure} therefore gives the limiting
logarithmic moment generating function
\[
 \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m
 \log\mathbb E e^{tmY_m^R}=P(t)-\log\varphi.
 \label{eq:standard-layer-log-mgf}
\]
The translated extremal theorem in the Fibonacci-convention lemma gives
\[
 \max_{n\in\mathcal I_m}\frac1m\log R(n)
 \longrightarrow\alpha_+.
 \label{eq:standard-layer-maximum}
\]
Indeed, the two shifted endpoints have partition value one, so they do not
change the maximum on the intervening Fibonacci block.  Thus the variables
are exponentially tight, and the G\"artner--Ellis upper bound gives the
required upper bound for closed sets.

For the lower bound, fix \(t>t_c=-\sigma_0\), write \(v=P'(t)>v_c\), and
tilt the uniform law on \(\mathcal I_n\) by \(R(k)^t\).  Analyticity of \(P\)
and the exponential Markov inequality applied to
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-pressure} show that this tilted law assigns
exponentially vanishing mass to
\[
 \left\{k\in\mathcal I_n:
   \left|n^{-1}\log R(k)-v\right|\ge\delta\right\}.
\]
Consequently, if
\[
 A_n^R(v,\delta)
 :=\card{\{k\in\mathcal I_n:
 |n^{-1}\log R(k)-v|<\delta\}},
\]
then
\begin{equation}
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log A_n^R(v,\delta)
 \ge P(t)-tv-|t|\delta.
 \label{eq:standard-layer-exposed-source}
\end{equation}

For large \(n\), every point counted here has multiplicity greater than two.
Each generating orbit contributes at most two points to \(\mathcal I_n\).
Keeping one generator from each represented orbit therefore produces a set
\(\mathcal G_n^R\) with
\[
 \card{\mathcal G_n^R}\ge\tfrac12A_n^R(v,\delta),
 \qquad L(g)\le n\quad(g\in\mathcal G_n^R),
\]
and \(n^{-1}\log R(g)\in(v-\delta,v+\delta)\).

Fix \(0<\alpha<\alpha_+\), set \(\rho=\alpha/v<1\), and put
\(n_m=\lfloor\rho m\rfloor\).  For \(g\in\mathcal G_{n_m}^R\), define
\[
 z_m(g)=
 \begin{cases}
  [a]g,&m-L(g)=2a,\\
  [a]\tau g,&m-L(g)=2a+1.
 \end{cases}
\]
The orbit formulas give \(L(z_m(g))=m\) and
\(R(z_m(g))=R(g)\), so \(z_m(g)\in\mathcal I_m\).  Freeness makes this map
injective.  From \eqref{eq:standard-layer-exposed-source}, first letting
\(\delta\downarrow0\), we obtain the local counting lower bound
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\liminf_{m\to\infty}\frac1m
 \log A_m^R(\alpha,\varepsilon)
 \ge\frac{\alpha}{v}\bigl(P(t)-tv\bigr).
 \label{eq:standard-layer-orbit-filling}
\end{equation}

If \(0<\alpha\le v_c\), let \(t\downarrow t_c\) in
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-orbit-filling}; then \(v\downarrow v_c\) and the
right side tends to \(\sigma_0\alpha\).  If
\(v_c<\alpha<\alpha_+\), let \(t_\alpha\) be the unique point with
\(P'(t_\alpha)=\alpha\), and let \(t\downarrow t_\alpha\) through values with
\(P'(t)>\alpha\).  The right side then tends to
\(P(t_\alpha)-t_\alpha\alpha\).  In both cases this is
\(\log\varphi-I(\alpha)\).  Division by
\(\card{\mathcal I_m}=F_m\) proves the LDP lower bound at every interior
point of the effective domain.

The effective domain and the affine formula follow from the same convex-dual
calculation as in \Cref{thm:full-fiber-ldp}, since the pressure is the same.
At \(0\), the left endpoint \(F_{m+1}-1\) has \(R=1\) and supplies the lower
bound directly.  At \(\alpha_+\), use interior points tending to the endpoint
and continuity of the finite convex rate on \([0,\alpha_+]\).  Hence the
lower bound holds for every open set, completing the full LDP.
\end{proof}

For the same uniform point \(\mathbf N_m\), retain the generating cost by
putting
\[
 B_m^R:=\frac1m\ell_m^R(\mathbf N_m).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Joint generation-cost and multiplicity large deviations]
\label{thm:joint-cost-reward-ldp}
The vectors \((B_m^R,Y_m^R)\) satisfy a full large deviation principle on
\(\mathbb R^2\) with speed \(m\).  Their limiting logarithmic moment generating
function is
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda(a,t)=\max\{0,a+P(t)\}-\log\varphi
 \qquad(a,t\in\mathbb R).
 \label{eq:joint-cost-reward-log-mgf}
\end{equation}
The good rate function is
\begin{equation}
 J(\beta,\alpha)=
 \begin{cases}
  (1-\beta)\log\varphi+\beta I(\alpha/\beta),
   &0<\beta\le1,\quad 0\le\alpha\le\beta\alpha_+,\\
  \log\varphi,&(\beta,\alpha)=(0,0),\\
  +\infty,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:joint-cost-reward-rate}
\end{equation}
In particular, the rate surface has the affine face
\begin{equation}
 J(\beta,\alpha)=\log\varphi-\sigma_0\alpha
 \qquad(0\le\beta\le1,\ 0\le\alpha\le\beta v_c).
 \label{eq:joint-cost-reward-flat-face}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let \(\mathcal W_j\) be the set of nonempty free generator words of total
cost \(j\), and write \(Q(w)\) for the product of the denominators of the
letters of \(w\).  By \eqref{eq:generator-weight-length},
\begin{equation}
 u_j(-t)=\sum_{w\in\mathcal W_j}Q(w)^t.
 \label{eq:fixed-cost-word-partition}
\end{equation}
The convention \(\ell_m^R=0\) at multiplicities at most two changes only a
bounded number of states.  The one-layer orbit weights in
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-marked-count} therefore give, up to a term bounded
for each fixed \((a,t)\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n\in\mathcal I_m}e^{a\ell_m^R(n)}R(n)^t
 =2\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}e^{aj}u_j(-t)+e^{am}u_m(-t)+O_{a,t}(1).
 \label{eq:joint-cost-reward-renewal}
\end{equation}
More precisely, the bounded correction removes the level-two generator orbit
from its positive cost and records it at cost zero; the level-one endpoint is
already a cost-zero term.

The proof of \Cref{thm:all-real-pressure} established
\(\lim_{j\to\infty}j^{-1}\log u_j(-t)=P(t)\), and \(u_j(-t)>0\) for every
\(j\ge8\).  Applying these facts to the partial sum in
\eqref{eq:joint-cost-reward-renewal}, while retaining the level-one endpoint,
gives
\[
 \lim_{m\to\infty}\frac1m\log
 \sum_{n\in\mathcal I_m}e^{a\ell_m^R(n)}R(n)^t
 =\max\{0,a+P(t)\}.
\]
Since \(\card{\mathcal I_m}=F_m\), this proves
\eqref{eq:joint-cost-reward-log-mgf}.

We next compute its convex dual.  For fixed \(t\),
\[
 \sup_{a\in\mathbb R}\{a\beta-\max(0,a+P(t))\}
 =\begin{cases}
   -\beta P(t),&0\le\beta\le1,\\
   +\infty,&\text{otherwise}.
  \end{cases}
\]
For \(0<\beta\le1\), subsequent optimization over \(t\) gives
\[
 \log\varphi+\beta\sup_t\{t\alpha/\beta-P(t)\}
 =(1-\beta)\log\varphi+\beta I(\alpha/\beta).
\]
At \(\beta=0\), the dual is finite only at \(\alpha=0\), where it equals
\(\log\varphi\).  The effective domain of \(I\) from
\Cref{thm:standard-fibonacci-thermodynamics} now gives exactly
\eqref{eq:joint-cost-reward-rate}.  The variables are supported in a common
compact set up to a vanishing enlargement: \(0\le B_m^R\le1\), and
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-maximum} bounds \(Y_m^R\).  Thus the
G\"artner--Ellis upper bound applies to every closed set.

It remains to prove the lower bound, because the interior points with
\(0<\beta<1\) are not exposed points of \(\Lambda\).  Fix \(t>t_c\) and put
\(v=P'(t)>v_c\).  On \(\mathcal W_j\), use the probability proportional to
\(Q(w)^t\).  For sufficiently small real \(s\),
\eqref{eq:fixed-cost-word-partition} and the fixed-cost pressure limit give
\[
 \limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac1j\log
 \Pr_t\left\{\left|j^{-1}\log Q(w)-v\right|\ge\delta\right\}<0.
\]
This is the same two-sided exponential Markov argument as in
\eqref{eq:standard-layer-exposed-source}.  Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \liminf_{j\to\infty}\frac1j\log
 \card{\{w\in\mathcal W_j:\ |j^{-1}\log Q(w)-v|<\delta\}}
 \ge P(t)-tv-|t|\delta.
 \label{eq:fixed-cost-exposed-source}
\end{equation}

Let \(0<\beta\le1\) and suppose first that
\(\alpha/\beta=v>v_c\).  Taking integers \(j_m\) with \(j_m/m\to\beta\),
each word counted in \eqref{eq:fixed-cost-exposed-source} determines at least
one point of \(\mathcal I_m\), because a generator orbit of cost \(j_m\le m\)
meets that layer.  Freeness preserves injectivity.  Hence the local counting
exponent is at least
\(\beta(P(t)-t\alpha/\beta)\), which, after division by \(F_m\), is
\(-J(\beta,\alpha)\).  The same conclusion at \(v_c\) follows by approximation
from \(t\downarrow t_c\).

Now let \(0<\alpha\le\beta v_c\).  Keep \(t>t_c\), put \(v=P'(t)\), and choose
integers
\[
 k_m/m\longrightarrow\alpha/v,\qquad j_m/m\longrightarrow\beta.
\]
Then \(r_m:=j_m-k_m\) tends linearly to infinity.  Every integer \(r\ge6\)
is the total cost of at most two letters with logarithmic multiplier
\(O(\log r)\): if \(r\) is odd, use the letter \(1/q\) with
\(q=(r+1)/2\), whose cost is \(2q-1=r\); if \(r\) is even, use \(1/2\) and
\(1/q\) with \(q=(r-2)/2\).  Append this fixed suffix to every source word of
cost \(k_m\) counted in \eqref{eq:fixed-cost-exposed-source}.  The free-monoid
property again gives an injection, the total cost is exactly \(j_m\), and the
added reward is \(o(m)\).  The resulting local counting exponent is at least
\[
 \frac{\alpha}{v}\{P(t)-tv\}.
\]
Letting \(t\downarrow t_c=-\sigma_0\), and using \(P(t)\to0\) and
\(v\downarrow v_c\), gives the exponent \(\sigma_0\alpha\).  For \(\alpha=0\)
the filler word alone gives exponent zero.  After normalization by \(F_m\),
these bounds equal the negative of
\eqref{eq:joint-cost-reward-flat-face}.  The point \((0,0)\) is supplied by
the level-one endpoint.  Finally, points with
\(\alpha=\beta\alpha_+\) follow by approximation from the relative interior;
the finite convex rate in \eqref{eq:joint-cost-reward-rate} is continuous on
its compact effective domain.  This proves the lower bound for every open set
and completes the full LDP.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Open second-order critical-cost interface]
\label{rem:critical-cost-tail-open-interface}
The preceding theorem uses only exponential rates and does not identify a
second-order critical renewal term.  Such a result would require, at a
minimum, a uniform mixed-denominator estimate of the form
\[
 \sum_{q\ge2}q^{-\sigma_0}
 \card{\{1\le p<q:(p,q)=1,\ c(p/q)>x\}}
 \sim K_Cx^{1-\sigma_0}\qquad(x\to\infty)
\]
with \(K_C>0\), including a proof that multiple large partial quotients and
possible lattice oscillations are lower order.  Fixed-denominator estimates
such as \cite{AistleitnerBordaHauke2023} do not by themselves provide the
uniform summation in the critical range \(q\asymp x\).  No such regular
variation statement, and hence no \(m^{3-\sigma_0}\) correction to the
critical partition sum, is asserted here.
\end{remark}

\begin{abstract}
This supplement collects three consequences outside the main article's
finite-window phase-transition spine: conditional microcanonical limits,
the exact dyadic multiplier law and its certified speed separation, and
integer-moment transfers with the direct quadratic recurrence.  It uses the
notation and numbered results of the main article.
\end{abstract}
\maketitle

\section{Conditional and dyadic arithmetic}
\label{sec:supplement-arithmetic}
The following results continue the marked-renewal analysis of the main
article.  Cross-references without a local target refer to \texttt{main.pdf}.
For \(x\in X_m\) with \(d_m(x)>2\), let \(g_m(x)\) be the unique Weinstein
generator of the orbit containing the corresponding partition integer, and
put
\[
 K_m(c)=
 \begin{cases}
 4,&c\le m-1,\\
 3,&c=m,\\
 1,&c=m+1,\\
 0,&c\ge m+2.
 \end{cases}
\]
